Semi-Global Existence and Trapped Surface Formation for the Einstein-Vlasov System
Nikolaos Athanasiou – Puskar Mondal – Shing-Tung Yau

Abstract
We prove a large-data semi-global existence theorem and the dynamical formation of trapped surfaces for the Einstein–massless Vlasov system in 3 + 1 3+1 dimensions, without any symmetry assumptions. The analysis critically hinges on a finely calibrated hierarchy of estimates for the Weyl curvature, Ricci coefficients, and velocity moments of the distribution function, designed to capture the delicate interaction between the geometric and kinetic structures of the coupled system. The presence of the Vlasov field introduces significant analytic challenges, both in terms of integrability and regularity. These are circumvented through a refined renormalization of the Ricci coefficients, a strategic restriction of the need for the elliptic estimates to a minimal number of Ricci coefficients and a precise commutator calculus adapted to the geometry of the mass-shell of the tangent bundle of the dynamical spacetime. The results constitute the first large-data, symmetry-free construction of dynamical black hole formation in the context of kinetic matter. Beyond its immediate application to the Einstein-Vlasov system in regimes of strong gravitational interaction and absence of symmetry, we anticipate that this framework will prove useful in a broader context, including, for instance, simplified approaches to the proof of nonlinear stability of Minkowski spacetime with massless Vlasov matter.

(3+1)-

\left(\mathcal{M},g\right)

X(f)=0,

\tilde{X}=p^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta}p^{\alpha}p^{\beta}\partial_{p^{\lambda}}\;\in\Gamma(TT\mathcal{M}).

1.1
Historical background

Trapped surfaces have been an object of significant interest within the classical theory of General Relativity for almost sixty years. After Schwarzschild’s discovery of his eponymous metric in 1915, it took almost twenty years before researchers came to realize the existence, within it, of a region
\mathcal{B}
with the following surprising yet salient features: First of all, observers situated inside
\mathcal{B}
cannot send signals to observers situated at an ideal
conformal boundary at infinity
, called
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
Furthermore, any observer located inside
\mathcal{B}
lives only for finite proper time
1
1

1

Sbierski
[
26
]
moreover showed that the termination of the observer’s proper time manifests in a particularly ferocious way, as they, in fact, get torn apart by infinitely strong tidal forces.
(geodesic incompleteness).
The characteristics of this region (which later came to be known as a black hole) took most of the researchers of the time aback. The consensus seemed to be that these observed phenomena have to be accidents; pathologies, only present because of the strong (spherical) symmetry inherent in the Schwarzschild solution and that, in general solutions to the Einstein equations
2
2

2

The meaning of this phrase was not rigorous at the time, as the setup for the initial value problem in General Relativity had not yet been discovered.
, such phenomena would not arise. However, in the 60’s, this belief was spectacularly falsified by Roger Penrose through his celebrated incompleteness theorem
3
3

3

This theorem, in fact, was the main reason why he was awarded the Nobel Prize in Physics back in 2020, ”for the discovery that black hole formation is a robust prediction of the general theory of relativity”.
. It was Penrose
[
23
]
who introduced the notion of a
trapped suface
, without which one cannot state his eponymous, celebrated, incompleteness theorem :

Definition 1

Given a
(3+1)
- dimensional Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
, a closed spacelike
2-
surface
S
is caled
trapped
if the following two fundamental forms
\chi
and
\underline{\chi}
have everywhere pointwise negative expansions on
S
:

\chi(X,Y):=g(D_{X}L,Y),\hskip 5.69054pt\underline{\chi}(X,Y):=g(D_{X}\underline{L},Y).

Here
D
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
g
,
L
and
\underline{L}
denote a null basis of the 2-dimensional orthogonal complement of
T_{p}S
in
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
, extended as smooth vector fields and
X,Y
are arbitrary
S-
tangent vector fields.

In other words, a surface is called trapped if both
\operatorname{tr}\chi
and
\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}
are pointwise negative everywhere on
S
. These traces signify the infinitesimal changes in area along the null generators normal to
S
, whence one can interpret trapped surfaces as closed, spacelike
2-
surfaces that infinitesimally decrease in area "along any possible future direction".

The incompleteness theorem is now presented.

Theorem 1.1
(Penrose Incompleteness)

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a spacetime containing a non-compact Cauchy hypersurface. If
(\mathcal{M},g)
moreover satisfies the null energy codition and contains a closed trapped surface, it is geodesically incomplete.

The existence of a trapped surface is a stable feature in the context of dynamics. Indeed, sufficiently small perturbations of Schwarzschild initial data must also contain such surfaces, by Cauchy stability. As such, incompleteness is not an accident, but rather a recurring theme in the dynamics of the Einstein equations.

At the time, the existence of a trapped surface in a spacetime was too strong an assumption to begin with. In fact, the only way back then to guarantee its existence was to assume it at the level of initial data, but this itself can be a highly non-trivial question. The first trapped surface formation result at the level of initial data was given
in
[
28
]
. Later,
[
29
]
provided a stronger result by proving the existence of a black hole at the level of the initial data set because of the boundary effect. In particular, this result did not require the inclusion of matter density (in fact, matter density could be allowed to be negative, even though such a case may be outside of physical interest; the latter two authors are pursuing this approach in the evolutionary framework). This, however, begs the question of whether trapped surfaces are dynamical objects, meaning whether they can be formed in evolution starting with data devoid of trapped surfaces. This problem bears high physical significance and serves as a
test of reality
of black holes, in the following sense. The mathematical definition of a black hole region would be without physical meaning if it did not accurately capture what physicists perceive as black holes (this is more meaningful now than ever, as scientists recently succeeded in capturing the first-ever image of a black hole). Hence if "mathematical" black holes describe "physical" black holes, they should mathematically verify certain physical properties, one of which is dynamical formation.

The first results along this direction were obtained by Christodoulou for the Einstein equations coupled to a massless scalar field in spherical symmetry. Through a series of works
[
12
]
,
[
10
]
,
[
11
]
and
[
14
]
, Christodoulou managed to not only prove trapped surface formation, but to understand the picture of gravitational collapse in its entirety for the given model and under the given symmetry. The breakthrough in the absence of symmetry came in
[
13
]
by the same author. In this work, Christodoulou introduced a hierarchy of small and large components in the initial data which (almost) persists under the evolution of the Einstein equations. He termed his method the
short pulse
method. After Christodoulou, the work
[
19
]
by Klainerman-Rodnianski reduces the size of Christodoulou’s work from about 600 to approximately 120 pages, by using a slightly different hierarchy. Moreover, it reduces the number of derivatives of curvature required to prove semi-global existence from two to one. A few years later, An
[
3
]
introduces the signature for decay rates
s_{2}
on his way to proving an extension of
[
19
]
from a finite region to a region close to past null infinity. In 2014, An and Luk
[
6
]
prove the first
scale-critical
trapped surface formation criterion for the vacuum equations in the absence of symmetry. While Christodoulou’s data in
[
13
]
were large in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, An and Luk give data which only have to be large in
H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which is a scale-critical norm for the initial data. Taking advantage of the scale criticality in
[
6
]
, An
[
2
]
constructs initial data that give rise not merely to trapped surfaces, but an
apparent horizon
, a smooth 3-dimensional hypersurface consisting of marginally outer trapped surfaces. In 2019, An
[
1
]
produces a 55-page proof of trapped surface formation for the vacuum equations, making use of the signature for decay rates and obtaining an existence result from a region close to past null infinity. In
[
4
]
, An and the first author extend
[
1
]
to the case of the Einstein-Maxwell system. Later, the present authors proved the large data scale-invariant semi-global existence and trapped surface formation result in
[
4
]
.

1.2
The Einstein-Vlasov System

First of all, we emphasize the physical importance of the Vlasov matter in general relativity. Most large-scale astrophysical structures- galaxies, galaxy clusters, dark matter halos are made of matter whose mean free path is extremely long. Particles (e.g. stars in a galaxy, or dark matter particles) interact predominantly via gravity, not collisions. Thus, the collisionless Vlasov equation is the natural model. The formation and stability of galaxies and clusters can be described via the Einstein–Vlasov (in the relativistic regime) or Vlasov–Poisson (in the Newtonian regime) equations. Lastly and most importantly, Vlasov matter provides a
clean
matter model (no artificial pressures, no exotic assumptions) that allows the study of whether self-gravitating distributions can undergo collapse, form singularities, or settle into equilibrium. It is central in rigorous mathematical relativity because:

a. It satisfies all standard energy conditions (dominant, strong, weak),

b. It avoids unphysical pathologies that occur in some fluid models,

c. It leads to nontrivial dynamics, all the while remaining mathematically tractable.

The Einstein–Vlasov system also models physically realistic systems (galaxies, dark matter). It thus constitutes a natural testing ground for the major conjectures of general relativity. It has been the subject of extensive investigation in recent decades. In the case of spherical symmetry, Rein and Rendall
[
24
]
-
[
25
]
established the global existence of small-data solutions. Subsequently, the first breakthrough in the absence of symmetry was made by Taylor
[
27
]
who proved the global nonlinear stability of Minkowski spacetime when coupled to massless (null) Vlasov matter. His proof employs the Sasaki metric on the mass shell together with estimates on Jacobi fields, and relies on small-data characteristic initial value theory. The argument further incorporates the vacuum stability results to conclude the global stability. Taylor’s work is of particular relevance here, since his analysis is carried out in double null gauge and yields gauge-invariant estimates for the Weyl curvature; our approach is in part motivated by this framework. Nevertheless, as we shall see, the large-data regime introduces substantial new difficulties. In subsequent work, Lindblad and Taylor
[
20
]
established the global stability of Minkowski spacetime for the massive Einstein–Vlasov system. Their analysis is conducted in spacetime harmonic coordinates, where the field equations reduce to a system of quasilinear wave equations for the metric satisfying the weak null condition, coupled to a transport equation for the particle distribution function. Simultaneously, Fajman, Joudioux, and Smulevici
[
18
]
extended these results significantly by proving stability without assuming compact support in the velocity variable, thereby broadening the admissible class of initial data.

In the context of gravitational collapse, Dafermos and Rendall
[
16
]
established the first rigorous result on black hole formation from the evolution of collisionless matter, proving that the collapse of Vlasov matter from suitably regular initial data in spherical symmetry leads to the formation of black holes. Further investigations of the Einstein–Vlasov system, both in asymptotically flat and cosmological settings (sometimes coupled with additional fields), can be found in
[
9
,
7
,
5
]
. The purpose of the present work is to go beyond symmetry-restricted settings by considering an open class of large characteristic initial data prescribed on past null infinity
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. This provides the first symmetry-free large-data analysis of the Einstein–Vlasov system and, in particular, establishes a dynamical trapped surface formation result. Our approach introduces several new techniques, including the replacement of Jacobi field methods with a direct analysis of commuted transport equations. The argument crucially exploits the null structure inherent in the commuted Vlasov equations. The main theorem, together with the underlying difficulties and innovations of the proof, will be described in the subsequent sections.

1.3
Main Theorem

In this section, we state our main results. Our first result is a semi-global existence result for the Einstein-Vlasov system for large data. The second, complementing the first, is a formation of trapped surfaces statement.

Theorem 1.2
(Semi–Global Existence and Trapped Surface Formation)

Fix a smooth double null foliation of a four–dimensional Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
with optical functions
(u,\underline{u})
such that the level sets
H_{u}
and
H_{\underline{u}}
are outgoing and incoming null hypersurfaces, respectively, and

S_{u,\underline{u}}:=H_{u}\cap H_{\underline{u}}

denotes the corresponding two–sphere sections equipped with the induced metric
\not{g}
. Let
\not{\nabla}
and
\not{\nabla}_{4}
denote the angular and null derivatives associated with the null frame

\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\},

and let
f
be a smooth nonnegative distribution function on the future mass–shell
\mathcal{P}\subset T\mathcal{M}
satisfying the Vlasov equation

X[f]=0,

where
X
is the geodesic spray.

(a) Semi–Global Existence.

For every fixed constant
\mathcal{I}>0
, there exists
a_{0}=a_{0}(\mathcal{I})\gg 1
such that the following holds.
Let
a>a_{0}
and let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large. Suppose the initial data set

(\hat{\chi}_{0},f_{0})

is prescribed along the characteristic hypersurfaces
H_{u_{\infty}}
and
\underline{H}_{0}
, where
u_{\infty}<0
is large in magnitude, and satisfies:

(Shear bound along
u=u_{\infty}
)

\sum_{0\leq i+m\leq N}a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|\not{\nabla}_{4}^{m}\big(|u_{\infty}|\not{\nabla}\big)^{i}\hat{\chi}_{0}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(S_{u_{\infty},\underline{u}})}\leq\mathcal{I},

for all
\underline{u}\in[0,1]
.

(Minkowskian incoming data)
All geometric data along
\underline{u}=0
coincide with the corresponding Minkowskian values and
and the Vlasov distribution vanishes:
f|_{\underline{u}=0}=0
.

(Momentum support and regularity of
f_{0}
)
The function
f_{0}
is smooth on the restriction
\mathcal{P}|_{u=u_{\infty}}
and obeys the uniform bound

\frac{1}{a}\sup_{u=u_{\infty}}\sum_{k=0}^{3}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}=1}^{7}\big|F_{i_{1}}\cdots F_{i_{k}}f_{0}\big|\leq\mathcal{I},

where
\{F_{1},\dots,F_{7}\}
denotes the orthonormal frame on
\mathcal{P}
defined in (
9.4
).
Moreover, its momentum support satisfies for fixed constants
C_{p^{A}},C_{p^{3}},C_{p^{4}}>0
independent of
u_{\infty}
,

0<p^{3}\leq C_{p^{3}},\qquad 0\leq|u|^{2}p^{4}\leq C_{p^{4}}p^{3},\qquad|u|^{2}|p^{A}|\leq C_{p^{A}}p^{3},\quad A=1,2,

(1.5)

for all
(x,p)\in\operatorname{supp}(f_{0})\cap\mathcal{P}_{u=u_{\infty}}
.

Then there exists a unique smooth solution
(g,f)
of the Einstein–Vlasov system in the region

\mathcal{D}:=\big\{(u,\underline{u})\mid u_{\infty}\leq u\leq-\tfrac{a}{4},\;\;0\leq\underline{u}\leq 1\big\},

satisfying the constraint and transport equations, with all geometric quantities
\psi\in\{\operatorname{tr}\chi,\hat{\chi},\underline{\hat{\chi}},\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi},\eta,\underline{\eta},\alpha,\underline{\beta},\rho,\sigma\}
remaining uniformly controlled by constants depending only on
(\mathcal{I},C_{p^{A}},C_{p^{3}},C_{p^{4}})
.

(b) Formation of a Trapped Surface.

Suppose, in addition to
(i)–(iii)
, that the initial energy flux along
C_{u_{\infty}}
satisfies the uniform lower bound

\int_{0}^{1}\Big(|u_{\infty}|^{2}|\hat{\chi}_{0}|^{2}+|u_{\infty}|\,T_{44}(u_{\infty},\underline{u}^{\prime})\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\underline{u}^{\prime}\geq a,

(1.6)

uniformly for every direction along
u=u_{\infty}
, where
T_{44}
denotes the energy–momentum density component associated with the Vlasov field. Then the outgoing null hypersurface
H_{-a/4}
possesses a closed two–sphere

S_{-a/4,\,1}=H_{-a/4}\cap\underline{H}_{1}

such that

\operatorname{tr}\chi(S_{-a/4,\,1})<0\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}(S_{-a/4,\,1})<0.

Hence
S_{-a/4,\,1}
is a trapped surface in
(\mathcal{M},g)
.

Remark 1

Notice that one can impose a weaker condition on the Vlasov data in terms of the integral over the null hypersurface
H_{u_{\infty}}
instead of a point-wise control, since that is exactly what we need in the ultimate estimates for the Vlasov equations. Nevertheless, the stronger assumption on the Vlasov initial data automatically implies the weaker integral bound. In addition the initial condition

0<p^{3}\leq C_{p^{3}},\hskip 5.69054pt0\leq\lvert u\rvert^{2}p^{4}\leq C_{p^{4}}p^{3},\hskip 5.69054pt\lvert u^{2}p^{A}\rvert\leq C_{p^{A}}p^{3},\hskip 5.69054pt\text{for}\hskip 1.42262ptA=1,2,

in
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\operatorname{supp}f\right|_{{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{P}\right|_{u=u_{\infty}}}}}
, for some fixed constants
C_{p_{1}},\dots,C_{p^{4}}
independent of
u_{\infty}
is precisely the
focusing
condition for the Vlasov matter.

1.4
Global estimates for the derivatives of
f
without the use of Jacobi fields

The first main contribution of this work concerns the delicate interplay between the regularity of the matter variables and the curvature components, as dictated by the contracted Bianchi identities. To set notation, let
\Psi
denote the collection of null components of the Weyl curvature tensor relative to a double null frame, and let
\mathcal{T}
denote the corresponding null components of the energy–momentum tensor induced by the Vlasov field. As is well known from the structure of the Bianchi equations, the source terms on the right-hand side involve derivatives of
\mathcal{T}
. Consequently, a priori estimates for
\Psi
necessitate control of one additional derivative of
\mathcal{T}
.

In the present framework, our analysis requires two derivatives of the curvature
\Psi
in order to close the nonlinear energy hierarchy. It therefore follows that one must establish uniform control of three derivatives of the Vlasov distribution function
f
. This forms the starting point of our regularity analysis.

We now recall the governing transport equation for the Vlasov distribution function. Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold, and denote by
P\mathcal{M}\subset T\mathcal{M}
the mass shell corresponding to massive or massless particles. The Vlasov distribution function is a nonnegative scalar function

f:T\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

assumed to be sufficiently regular and compactly supported in the momentum variables. It is transported along the geodesic flow, that is, it is invariant under the Liouville vector field
X
. Explicitly,

X[f]=0.

(1.7)

Let
\{e_{4},e_{3},e_{1},e_{2}\}
be a double null frame on
M
, with
e_{4},e_{3}
null and normalized so that
g(e_{3},e_{4})=-2
. Extend this frame by adjoining the natural coordinate derivatives in the momentum variables,
\{\partial_{p^{3}},\partial_{p^{1}},\partial_{p^{2}}\}
. Relative to this frame, the Liouville operator takes the form

X=p^{\mu}e_{\mu}-\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\lambda}p^{\nu}p^{\lambda}\partial_{p^{\mu}},

(1.8)

where
p^{\mu}
are the fiber coordinates on the tangent bundle, constrained by the mass shell relation
g_{\mu\nu}p^{\mu}p^{\nu}=-m^{2}
(with
m=0
in the massless case-which is the case here), and
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\lambda}
are the Christoffel symbols of
g
. Accordingly, the transport equation (
1.33
) reads in components:

p^{\mu}e_{\mu}(f)-\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\lambda}p^{\nu}p^{\lambda}\,\partial_{p^{\mu}}f=0.

(1.9)

Equation (
1.9
) is the precise analytic formulation of the free-streaming of Vlasov particles in curved spacetime. Its hyperbolic–transport structure is the fundamental mechanism by which derivatives of
f
interact with the connection coefficients, and thereby enter into the higher-order commutator estimates necessary for controlling the derivatives of the curvature tensor.
In order to obtain control of higher derivatives of the distribution function
f
, a natural idea is to commute the transport equation (
1.33
) with carefully chosen vector fields adapted to the null geometry and then integrate along the geodesic flow. Concretely, suppose
V
is such a commutation vector field. Then the commuted equation reads

X[Vf]=[X,V]f.

The error term
[X,V]f
contains contributions of the form
V[\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\lambda}]
, which, at the heuristic level, lie at the same regularity as the Weyl curvature components. However, as already emphasized in
[
27
]
, certain components of these commutators present significant technical difficulties.

For instance, terms of the form
V[\Gamma^{A}_{3B}]
involve derivatives of the shift vector
b
, since

\Gamma^{A}_{3B}=\underline{\chi}^{A}_{\;\;B}-e_{A}(b^{B}).

(1.10)

Thus,
V[\Gamma^{A}_{3B}]
generates terms with two derivatives of
b
. To estimate
b
, one relies on its transport equation,

\not{\nabla}_{4}b\sim\eta-\underline{\eta}+\chi\cdot b,

(1.11)

which couples
b
to the torsion coefficients
\eta,\underline{\eta}
and the null second fundamental form
\chi
.

As discussed, closing the energy estimates requires control of two derivatives of the Weyl curvature, which in turn necessitates control of three derivatives of
f
. Thus, one is led to commute the Vlasov transport equation (
1.33
) three times with
V
, schematically:

X[V^{3}f]=[X,V^{3}]f+.......

This produces error terms of the type
V^{3}(\Gamma^{A}_{3B})
, which, by (
1.10
), involve
four derivatives
of the shift
b
. However, the transport equation (
1.11
), together with available elliptic estimates on the connection coefficients, yields control of at most
three
derivatives. Hence a derivative gap of one order appears, obstructing the closure of the argument.

An analogous difficulty arises in controlling terms such as
V^{3}(\not{\Gamma}^{A}_{BC})
, where
\not{\Gamma}
denotes the Levi–Civita connection coefficients of the induced metric on the
S_{u,\underline{u}}
spheres. These coefficients obey an equation of the schematic form

\not{\nabla}_{4}\not{\Gamma}\sim\not{\nabla}\chi+\not{\Gamma}\cdot\Gamma,

(1.12)

so that
V^{3}(\not{\Gamma})
also requires control of four derivatives of
\chi
, which again exceeds the regularity threshold permitted in our framework.

Taylor
[
27
]
circumvented this derivative loss by avoiding direct commutation altogether. Instead, he introduced Jacobi fields associated with the geodesic flow, which permit control of higher derivatives of
f
without producing uncontrolled higher derivatives of the connection (note that the Jacobi fields are more fundamental than the shift vector
b
). One fundamental structural distinction between the stability problem and the problem of trapped surface formation concerns the analytical role played by Jacobi fields along the mass shell. In the global small-data regime considered by Taylor, the use of Jacobi fields is particularly well-suited to capturing the long-time dispersive decay of the Vlasov field. By contrast, in the regime of large, concentrated initial data—precisely the regime relevant to the formation of trapped surfaces-the same framework breaks down, essentially because the curvature and shear amplify rather than decay.

To recall, let
P\subset T\mathcal{M}
denote the future mass shell of the underlying spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g)
, equipped with the Sasaki metric. The geodesic spray on
P
generates the characteristic flow of the Vlasov equation. If
X
denotes the horizontal lift of the geodesic vector field, then a Jacobi field
J
along the flow satisfies the second-order variational equation

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{X}\widehat{\nabla}_{X}J\;=\;\widehat{R}(X,J)X,

(1.13)

where
\widehat{\nabla}
is the Levi–Civita connection induced by the Sasaki metric and
\widehat{R}
its associated curvature tensor. The Jacobi fields thus encode the infinitesimal linearization of the geodesic flow on phase space and constitute the principal tool for propagating regularity of the distribution function
f
.

In the small-data regime of perturbations of Minkowski spacetime, the ambient curvature remains uniformly weak. Consequently, the right-hand side of the Jacobi equation acts only as a perturbative source term, and Jacobi fields exhibit at most mild growth along the flow. Taylor exploits this boundedness to propagate uniform estimates for the derivatives of
f
. This, in turn, yields the dispersive decay of the matter terms in the energy–momentum tensor, which is the cornerstone of the global stability analysis.

The situation is fundamentally altered in the large-data regime relevant for collapse, more precisely, in the focusing regime where the initial data is prescribed on the past null infinity
u=u_{\infty}
. When the initial data are concentrated so as to encode incoming gravitational radiation of sufficiently large amplitude, the nonlinear interaction of curvature and shear leads to the strong focusing of null geodesics (even though the analysis in the double null framework aims to prove no occurrence of caustics before a trapped surface can form, but this needs to be proved, not assumed). From the point of view of the Jacobi equation, heuristically, this corresponds to the amplification of
\widehat{R}(X,\cdot)X
, so that solutions
J
can undergo rapid contraction. In geometric terms, Jacobi fields, instead of providing a stable linearization, degenerate. Thus, the very mechanism on which Taylor relies-the uniform boundedness of Jacobi fields and the consequent control of derivatives of
f
breaks down. Let us make this idea more concrete. For the estimates necessary to prove global control of the distribution function
f
, the Jacobi fields must verify the following estimates (see section
9
for details: essentially, the Jacobi fields are designed so that they produce the same control as the constructed vector fields
V
in section
9
) for
s\in[-s_{*},0]
and
u=u(s),~u(0)=u_{\infty}
and
u(-s_{*})=-\frac{a}{4}
i.e.,
\pi\bigg(\exp_{-s^{*}}(x,p)\bigg)\in\{u=-\frac{a}{4}\}

\displaystyle|J^{B}_{(A)}(s)-|u(s)|\delta^{B}_{A}|\leq C,~A,B=1,2,~~|J^{3}_{3}(s)-|u(s)||\leq C,~|J^{4}_{4}(s)-1|\leq\frac{C}{|u(s)|},

(1.14)

\displaystyle|J^{j}_{(i)}(s)|\leq C,~i=1-6,j=1-4,~|J^{4+B}_{(A)}(s)-\delta^{B}_{A}p^{3}(s)|\leq\frac{Cp^{3}(s)}{|u(s)|},

(1.15)

\displaystyle|J^{4+A}_{(i)}(s)|\leq\frac{Cp^{3}(s)}{|u(s)|},

(1.16)

for some small constant
C
so that the Jacobi fields are uniformly estimated. The first problem appears in understanding the initial condition for the Jacobi fields. In particular, in order to obtain the appropriate boundedness statements, certain cancellations must occur in certain terms arising from
J^{i}(0)
and certain terms arising from
(\widehat{\nabla}_{X}J)^{i}(0)
for some of the Jacobi fields. At this stage,
u_{\infty}
appears in defining the initial condition for
J
, which leads to an obstruction in obtaining uniform estimates for the Jacobi fields throughout the domain of existence. This is primarily due to the fact that
s
behaves like
|u|-|u_{\infty}|
.

\displaystyle\bigg|J^{4+A}(s)-\left(J^{4+A}(0)+s\frac{dJ^{k}\Xi^{4+A}_{k}}{ds}(s=0)\right)\bigg|\leq\frac{Cp^{3}}{|u(s)|^{2}}\sum_{B=1}^{2}|J^{B}(0)+s\frac{dJ^{k}\Xi^{B}_{k}}{ds}(0)|

(1.17)

\displaystyle+\frac{Cp^{3}}{|u(s)|}\sum_{i=3}^{4}|J^{i}(0)+s\frac{dJ^{k}\Xi^{i}_{k}}{ds}(s=0)|+\frac{C}{|u(s)|}\sum_{i=5}^{7}|J^{i}(0)+s\frac{dJ^{k}\Xi^{i}_{k}}{ds}(0)|

\displaystyle+C\int_{s}^{0}|\left(\frac{d(J^{k}(s^{{}^{\prime}})\Xi_{k}^{4+A}(s^{{}^{\prime}}))}{ds^{{}^{\prime}}}-\frac{d(J^{k}\Xi_{k}^{4+A})}{ds^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}(s^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=0)\right)|ds^{{}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{|u(s)|}\sum_{j=1}^{7}\int_{s}^{0}|\left(\frac{d(J^{k}(s^{{}^{\prime}})\Xi_{k}^{j}(s^{{}^{\prime}}))}{ds^{{}^{\prime}}}-\frac{d(J^{k}\Xi_{k}^{j})}{ds^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}(s^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=0)\right)|ds^{{}^{\prime}}

Now, we need to control
J^{I}(0)+s\frac{dJ^{k}\Xi^{I}_{k}}{ds}(s=0)
. Note that since
s\sim|u|-|u_{\infty}|
, one ought to expect some cancellation. otherwise
\frac{s}{|u(s)|^{1,2}}
would not be under control. This is very tricky in the current framework. Even though one can overcome this problem, there are additional problems that are inherent in the large data regime. The main challenge is to control the integrals of the type

\int_{s}^{0}\bigg|\left(\frac{d(J^{k}(s^{{}^{\prime}})\Xi_{k}^{j}(s^{{}^{\prime}}))}{ds^{{}^{\prime}}}-\frac{d(J^{k}\Xi_{k}^{j})}{ds^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}(s^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=0)\right)\bigg|ds^{{}^{\prime}}.

Analytically, one may summarize this as follows. In the stability problem, Jacobi fields are propagated from a small-data initial null hypersurface towards outgoing null infinity; the weak curvature along this direction enforces dispersion, so the distribution function spreads in both position and momentum space, and the corresponding stress-energy decays. In contrast, in the large-data problem of trapped surface formation, one has geometric control of Jacobi fields only at null infinity and must propagate them along incoming null geodesics, precisely the regime in which curvature induces focusing. In this setting, rather than dispersing, the Vlasov density remains concentrated, reinforcing curvature growth and leading to collapse. The Jacobi field method, being intrinsically adapted to dispersive regimes, is not
straightforwardly adaptable
to the issue of controlling this concentration.

Let us now formulate the heuristic argument in a more precise manner.
In the stability problem for Minkowski space, the central analytic task is to control Jacobi fields along the outgoing null direction
v
, given initial data prescribed on a null hypersurface
\{v=v_{0}\}
. The prescribed data on initial incoming and outgoing null hypersurfaces, consisting of appropriately weighted norms of Weyl curvature components, Ricci coefficients, and matter contributions, are assumed to satisfy a smallness condition. Within this framework, control of the Jacobi fields is obtained by integrating the Jacobi equation (
1.13
).

For concreteness, consider the Jacobi field
J^{B}_{(A)}
,
A,B=1,2
. One seeks an estimate of the form

\big|J^{A}{}_{(B)}(s)-\delta^{A}_{B}\,|u(s)|\big|\;\leq\;C(\overline{C}),

(1.18)

for
s\in[-s_{*},0]
and
C(\overline{C})\to 0
as
\overline{C}\to 0
. In our context, this constant
C
should preferably contain
a^{-\gamma},~\gamma>0
, which would yield the desired smallness. However, this does not seem to be possible. In the case of a stability problem, the initial conditions for
J_{(B)}
are given on
\{v=v_{\infty}\}
, i.e., at a large outgoing coordinate close to infinity, and
\overline{C}
denotes the smallness of the data (cf. Taylor
[
27
]
). In the current framework, the equivalence would be that the initial condition for the Jacobi field is calculated at
u=u_{\infty}
.

To establish (
1.18
), one introduces the bootstrap assumption

\bigg|\frac{d}{ds}\Big(J^{k}(s)\,\Theta^{j}_{k}(s)\Big)-\frac{d}{ds}\Big(J^{k}\,\Theta^{j}_{k}\Big)(0)\bigg|\;\leq\;\frac{C_{1}a^{\frac{1}{2}}\,p^{3}(s)}{|u(s)|^{2}},

(1.19)

for some constant
C_{1}>0
, where
\Theta^{j}_{k}
denotes the frame transformation matrix. Notice that in our framework,
p^{3}
does not decay at all throughout the domain of existence. Under (
1.19
), a straightforward calculus argument yields (
1.18
) for a constant
C=C(C_{1})
through (
1.17
).

The crucial step is to verify (
1.19
) by integrating the Jacobi equation (
1.13
). Since the equation is sourced by the curvature of the mass shell, one obtains

\int_{s}^{0}\Big|(\widehat{R}(X,J),X)^{i}\,\Theta^{j}_{i}(s^{\prime})\Big|\,ds^{\prime}\;\leq\;\frac{\overline{C}C(C_{1})a^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\,p^{3}(s)}{|u(s)|^{2}}.

(1.20)

The idea would be to obtain
\overline{C}
to be small.
Here, the factor
\overline{C}
arises from the smallness of the initial data and ensures that the contribution of the curvature source is sufficiently suppressed. By choosing
\overline{C}
small relative to
C_{1}
, one obtains an improvement of the bootstrap bound (
1.19
), thereby closing the argument. Now in our framework, recall

\displaystyle\text{Ver}_{(x,p)}(R(p,e_{A})p)=-\frac{1}{2}\bigg[-p^{4}p^{3}(2\beta_{A}+T_{4A})+p^{3}p^{3}(-2\underline{\beta}_{A}+T_{A3})+p^{C}p^{3}(\rho\not{g}_{AC}+3\sigma\epsilon_{AC}

\displaystyle+T_{AC}-\frac{1}{2}\not{g}_{AC}T_{43})-p^{4}p^{C}(\alpha_{CA}+\frac{1}{2}\not{g}_{AC}T_{44})+p^{C}p^{B}(-\not{g}_{CB}\beta_{A}+\not{g}_{AC}\beta_{B}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\not{g}_{CB}T_{4A}-\frac{1}{2}\not{g}_{AC}T_{4B})\bigg]\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{3}}+\dots

(1.21)

and similarly one can write down
\text{Hor}_{x,p}(R(p,e_{A})p)
as well.
The vital point to note here is that this decay can not be improved (at least in the scaling framework that we are considering) due to the presence of
\alpha
in the curvature term that exhibits the slowest decay
|\alpha|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|}
and appearance of
\underline{\beta}p^{3}p^{3}
where
p^{3}
does not have any decay and
|\underline{\beta}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{3}{2}}}{|u|^{3}}
. In particular, one obtains

\int_{s}^{0}\Big|(\widehat{R}(X,J),X)^{i}\,\Theta^{j}_{i}(s^{\prime})\Big|\,ds^{\prime}\;\leq\;\frac{\overline{C}\,C(C_{1})a^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{3}(s)}{|u(s)|^{2}}+\frac{\overline{C}C(C_{1})a^{\frac{3}{2}}p^{3}(s)}{|u(s)|^{2}}.

(1.22)

A second integration of (
1.22
) would lead to a large constant that would ruin the estimates for (
1.17
).
It is possible that this decay could be closed by considering a completely different scaling hierarchy of the norms.

In the large–data regime, the factor
\overline{C}
can no longer be regarded as small and since the decay can not be improved we do not gain an extra
a^{-\kappa},\kappa>0
smallness factor ((in fact in the current large data framework factors such as
a^{\gamma},\gamma>0
appears which is large)), and the integral estimate (
1.20
) no longer furnishes a bootstrap improvement. There is a good reason for this: in Section
9
, we will see that appropriate normalization of
V^{k}f
is needed to obtain uniform estimates in the large data regime, which is not immediate from the direct use of the Jacobi field. In particular, the curvature source contributes at the same or worse scale as the bootstrap threshold, preventing closure. Thus, while the Jacobi field method suffices in the perturbative (small–data) setting, its extension to the large–data regime encounters a genuine obstruction. This indicates that a fundamentally new mechanism is required to treat the characteristic Cauchy problem in the large-data context. While the failure of closure seems to preclude a direct extension of the Jacobi field to the large data framework as in ours, it remains very much conceivable that a new scaling involving the Jacobi field and the Weyl curvature could provide a resolution to the problem. But we pursue a completely different novel technique here that is more direct and conceptually simpler.

Our method introduces a genuinely new analytic framework for the Einstein–Vlasov system. It not only streamlines the derivation of higher-order estimates but, more importantly, provides a systematic avenue for addressing the large-data regime. This encompasses not only the problem of trapped surface formation but also a broader class of dynamical phenomena in the large data regime.

The central innovation lies in the identification of an adapted system of commutation vector fields and the concomitant development of a commutator calculus intrinsically suited to the null geometric structure of the problem. A key observation is that the commutators of the form

[X,V]

generate derivatives of the connection coefficients exclusively through antisymmetric combinations. Concretely, the fundamental identity reads

e_{\mu}(\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\nu})-e_{\beta}(\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu})=R^{\alpha}_{\;\;\nu\mu\beta}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\beta\nu}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\lambda}+\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\lambda}+\bigl(\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\beta}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\beta\mu}\bigr)\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\lambda\nu},

(1.23)

so that derivatives of the connection never appear in isolation, but only in the curvature combination (
1.23
), together with quadratic lower-order terms. Let us provide a concrete example of how the issue of both regularity and integrability is miraculously restored. Consider, for example,
V=V_{(A)}=\text{Hor}(e_{A})-\frac{p^{3}}{\lvert u\rvert}\partial_{\overline{p}^{A}}
(see section
9
for the definition of the commuting
V
vector fields) and observe the following in the standard basis (see section
9
for the exact definition):

\displaystyle[X,Hor(e_{A})-\frac{p^{3}}{\lvert u\rvert}\partial_{\overline{p}^{A}}]^{4+B}

\displaystyle=-X(\Gamma^{B}_{A\nu}p^{\nu})+Hor(e_{A})(\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu}p^{\mu}p^{\nu})-X\left(\frac{p^{3}}{\lvert u\rvert}{\delta_{A}}^{B}\right)-\frac{p^{3}}{\lvert u\rvert}\partial_{\overline{p}^{A}}(\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu}p^{\mu}p^{\nu})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(e_{A}(\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu})-e_{\mu}(\Gamma^{B}_{A\nu}))\hskip 1.42262ptp^{\mu}p^{\nu}}_{\text{I}}+\Gamma^{C}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.42262ptp^{\mu}\hskip 1.42262ptp^{\nu}\partial_{\overline{p}^{C}}(p^{\lambda})\Gamma^{B}_{A\lambda}+\Gamma^{3}_{\mu\nu}p^{\mu}p^{\nu}\partial_{\overline{p}^{3}}(p^{\lambda})\Gamma^{B}_{A\lambda}

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\Gamma^{C}_{A\lambda}p^{\lambda}\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\overline{p}^{C}}(p^{\mu}p^{\nu})}_{\text{II}}-\Gamma^{3}_{A\lambda}p^{\lambda}\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\overline{p}^{3}}(p^{\mu}p^{\nu})+\left(\underbrace{-\frac{(p^{3})^{2}}{\lvert u\rvert^{2}}{\delta_{A}}^{B}}_{\text{I}}+\frac{{\delta_{A}}^{B}}{\lvert u\rvert}\Gamma^{3}_{\mu\nu}p^{\mu}p^{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\frac{p^{3}}{\lvert u\rvert}\partial_{\overline{p}^{A}}(p^{\mu}p^{\nu})\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu}}_{\text{II}}.

(1.24)

Each pair of terms in brackets labelled (I) and (II) leads to a cancellation that improves the order of decay. Indeed, the first term in bracket (II), is
o(\lvert u\rvert^{-3})-{\underline{\chi}_{A}}^{C}\hskip 1.42262ptp^{3}\hskip 1.42262pt\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.42262pt\partial_{\overline{p}^{C}}(p^{\mu}p^{\nu})=-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}\hskip 1.42262ptp^{3}\hskip 1.42262pt\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu}\hskip 1.42262pt\partial_{\overline{p}^{A}}({p^{\mu}p^{\nu}})+o(\lvert u\rvert^{-3})
. By itself, this term is only
o(\lvert u\rvert^{-2})
. When combined, however, with the second term in bracket
(II)
, the whole bracket becomes

(II)=-\frac{1}{2}p^{3}\hskip 1.42262pt\big(\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{2}{\lvert u\rvert}\big)\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\overline{p}^{A}}(p^{\mu}p^{\nu})+o(\lvert u\rvert^{-3})=o(\lvert u\rvert^{-3}).

As for the terms in bracket (I), it is here where not only the issue of integrability, but also the issue of regularity in the terms is overcome. Indeed, notice that

e_{A}(\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\nu})-e_{\mu}(\Gamma^{B}_{A\nu})={R^{B}}_{\nu A\mu}-\Gamma^{\epsilon}_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{B}_{A\epsilon}+\Gamma^{\epsilon}_{A\nu}\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\epsilon}+(\Gamma^{\epsilon}_{A\mu}-\Gamma^{\epsilon}_{\mu A})\Gamma^{B}_{\epsilon\nu}.

(1.25)

In other words, throughout the entire argument,
derivatives of Christoffel symbols will always appear in differences that give rise to Riemann curvature components (up to terms of the form
\Gamma\Gamma
, which are harmless, both in terms of integrability and regularity)
.

As a consequence, the first term in
(I)
is
o(\lvert u\rvert^{-3})+\Gamma^{\epsilon}_{A\nu}\Gamma^{B}_{\mu\epsilon}p^{\mu}p^{\nu}
, where the latter fails to be
o(\lvert u\rvert^{-3})
precisely when
\mu=\nu=3
. In that case, the worst term is

\Gamma^{C}_{A3}\Gamma^{B}_{3C}p^{3}p^{3}\hskip 2.84526pt\text{(summation over C=1,2)}=\frac{1}{4}(\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi})^{2}(p^{3})^{2}=o(\lvert u\rvert^{-2}).

When combined, however, with the second term in bracket (I), the whole bracket becomes

(I)=\frac{1}{4}(p^{3})^{2}\big(\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{2}{\lvert u\rvert}\big)\big(\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}-\frac{2}{\lvert u\rvert}\big){\delta_{A}}^{B}+o(\lvert u\rvert^{-3})=o(\lvert u\rvert^{-3}).

Moreover,
in this framework the quantities
e_{A}(b^{B})
never arise independently, but always in the coupled form with
\underline{\chi}^{A}_{B}
, thereby combining into the connection coefficient
\Gamma^{A}_{3B}
associated with the endomorphism structure of the tangent bundle of the leaves
S_{u,\underline{u}}
. This structural cancellation is decisive: once the commutation vector fields
V
are aligned with the null frame, the apparent derivative loss is eliminated and a significant reduction in the associated elliptic estimates is achieved. This is novel, and we discuss this in the subsequent sections.

As a consequence, curvature terms can be controlled precisely at the target order, while the quadratic connection contributions, being effectively of lower differential order, enjoy stronger decay. These improved decay properties ensure that no obstruction to integrability emerges in the higher-order energy estimates.

The development of this commutator calculus in the adapted
V
-basis (introduced systematically in Chapter
9
) constitutes one of the main novelties of our method. Beyond its immediate application to the Einstein-Vlasov system in regimes of strong gravitational interaction and absence of symmetry, we anticipate that this framework will prove useful in a broader context, including, for instance, simplified approaches to the proof of nonlinear stability of Minkowski spacetime with massless Vlasov matter.

To summarize, our new approach yields a novel framework in the large data and symmetry-free regime (which is also conceptually simple) that avoids the derivative loss and provides additional technical advantages: a reduction in the reliance on elliptic estimates, sharp control at the optimal regularity level, and greater transparency in the energy hierarchy. We will exploit these features systematically in the sections that follow.

1.5
Fundamental analytic obstruction in the characteristic treatment of the Vlasov system in the absence of symmetries

Beyond the high–order regularity issues discussed in the preceding subsection, a principal analytic obstruction in the present characteristic formulation of the Einstein–Vlasov system is the lack of
top–order
control of the Vlasov field along the incoming null hypersurface. In the framework under consideration, the characteristic initial value problem is posed with nontrivial data prescribed on a past outgoing null hypersurface
\mathcal{H}_{u_{\infty}}
asymptotic to past null infinity, and
trivial
geometric data induced from Minkowski space on the intersecting past incoming null hypersurface
\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\underline{u}_{\infty}}
.

Unlike the pure vacuum setting or with other matter models such as Einstein-Maxwell, Einstein-Yang-Mills, or Einstein-Scalar system, where one can avoid the need to estimate all derivatives (derivatives tangential to the spheres as well as null derivatives) of the Ricci coefficients, the Weyl curvature components, and the stress-energy tensors if the regularity level is assumed to be sufficiently high. This is different in the case of the Vlasov matter. Heuristically speaking, the Vlasov stress-energy tensor

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x,p)\,p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\;\frac{1}{p^{3}}\,\sqrt{\det\not{g}}\,dp^{1}dp^{2}dp^{3}.

(1.26)

is a non-local entity in the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{M}
of the physical spacetime
\mathcal{M}
. In particular, the distribution function
f
is a function on the mass-shell
P\subset T\mathcal{M}
, a seven-dimensional variety of the tangent bundle. Therefore, in order to obtain higher order estimates for
f
, one needs to devise the vector fields
V
defined on the mass shell or a section of the double tangent bundle
TT\mathcal{M}
. Any appropriate vector field that verifies the regularity closure property, as illustrated in the previous section, would contain every derivative in its projection onto
T\mathcal{M}
. Now, in the higher order estimates (
\geq 2
) for the Vlasov distribution function
f
through commuting the transport equation

\displaystyle X[f]=0,

(1.27)

as we shall observe in section
9
, would produce derivatives of the connection coefficients and Weyl curvature components along every direction
e_{A},e_{4},e_{3}
. This necessitates the control of every derivative of the Ricci coefficients and the Weyl curvature components, irrespective of the regularity level under consideration. This complicates the analysis and requires careful renormalization of Weyl curvature and Ricci coefficients at appropriate stages. Moreover, this necessitates treating
|u|
as a weighted function with signature
s_{2}(|u|)=-1
and
s_{2}(|u|^{-1})=+1
. As we shall explain in later sections, this causes an apparent obstruction at the top order due to the fact that the vlasov source can only be controlled on
H
at the top order in this framework.

Since the Vlasov equation is a transport equation along null geodesics in the mass shell, it admits integration along incoming null geodesics provided one has a suitable control on the relation between the affine parameter
s
and the optical function
u
. Concretely, if
\gamma(s)
denotes the projection to the spacetime manifold of the null geodesic in the mass–shell generated by the geodesic spray
X
, then

\frac{p^{3}(s)}{\frac{du}{ds}}=1,

(1.28)

where
u(s):=u(\gamma(s))
and
(p^{4},p^{3},p^{1},p^{2})
denote the components of the momentum in the chosen double–null frame
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\}
, with
e_{3}
tangent to
\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\underline{u}}
and
e_{4}
tangent to
\mathcal{H}_{u}
.

In this gauge, the zero–th order bootstrap bounds for the momentum components take the form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\,p^{3}(0)\ \leq\ p^{3}(s)\ \leq\ 2\,p^{3}(0),

(1.29)

\displaystyle 0\ \leq\ |u(s)|^{2}\,p^{4}(s)\ \leq\ 2\,C_{p^{4}}\,p^{3}(0),

(1.30)

\displaystyle|u(s)|^{2}\,|p^{A}(s)|\ \leq\ 2\,C_{p^{A}}\,p^{3}(0),

(1.31)

for constants
C_{p^{4}},C_{p^{A}}>0
fixed by the initial data, where indices
A\in\{1,2\}
refer to the angular momentum components if the initial momentum component
p^{3}(0)
satisfies

C_{1}\ <\ p^{3}(0)\ \leq\ C_{2},~0\leq|u_{\infty}|^{2}p^{4}(u_{\infty})\leq Cp^{3}(0),~|u_{\infty}|^{2}\,|p^{A}(u_{\infty})|\ \leq\ 2\,C_{p^{A}}\,p^{3}(0)

(1.32)

for constants
0<C_{1}<C_{2}<\infty
.

The Vlasov equation in geometric form reads

X[f]=0,

(1.33)

where
X
is the geodesic spray vector field on the mass–shell. Bounds (
1.28
)–(
1.31
) imply that (
1.33
) admits direct integration only along the
incoming
(
u
–direction) null geodesics. However, in the coupled Einstein–Vlasov system, the energy–momentum tensor

T_{\alpha\beta}(x)=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}f(x,p)\,p_{\alpha}\,p_{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}(p)

enters as a source term in the transport equations for the Ricci coefficients. Estimating these Ricci coefficients therefore requires integration in
both
the
u
– and
\underline{u}
–directions.

For instance, the Raychaudhuri equation for
\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}
takes the form

\not{\nabla}_{3}\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi})^{2}=-|\hat{\chi}|^{2}-2\,\underline{\omega}\,\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}-T_{33}.

(1.34)

To control
\|\not{\nabla}^{2}\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}\|_{L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})}
, one must commute (
1.34
) with angular derivatives and thus estimate
\not{\nabla}^{2}T_{33}
in
L^{2}(\underline{H}_{\underline{u}})
. By the definition of
T_{33}
and (
1.33
), this necessitates
L^{2}(\underline{H}_{\underline{u}})L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{x})
–control of two derivatives of
f
on
\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\underline{u}}
, whereas (
1.28
) only permits direct control of such norms along
\mathcal{H}_{u}
.

Consequently, a naive approach fails: top–order angular derivatives of
T_{33}
cannot be propagated along
\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\underline{u}}
using only (
1.33
). The resolution lies in identifying the
correct functional norms
of
f
to be propagated, and determining
which submanifolds of the mass–shell
those norms should be estimated on. In the present setting—where control of two derivatives of the Weyl curvature is required—one must control
three
derivatives of
f
. Crucially, the derivatives of
f
appear in different norms over different hypersurfaces:
Controlling
\not{\nabla}^{2}\not{T}_{33}
in
L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})
requires
f\in L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{x})
.
To bound
V^{2}f
(with
V
one of the commutation vector fields) in
L^{2}(S_{u,\underline{u}})L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{x})
, one needs uniform
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{x})
control of
Vf
over
S_{u,\underline{u}}
.
This in turn implies the need to control
V^{3}f
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{u})L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{x})
, a norm that
is
directly accessible from (
1.33
).

In this way, different derivative orders of
f
are controlled on distinct families of null hypersurfaces in the mass–shell, and the principal technical challenge is to propagate these norms in a manner compatible with the geometry of the characteristic initial value problem.

An additional obstruction appears at the elliptic estimate stage: In the Einstein equations, elliptic estimates on
S_{u,\underline{u}}
are employed to control top–order angular derivatives of the Ricci coefficients. For “good” Ricci coefficients, this is compatible with the Vlasov propagation, but for “bad” coefficients such as
\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}
, elliptic estimates would require
L^{2}(\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\underline{u}})L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{x})
–control of
V^{3}f
, which is not directly available from (
1.33
).

Our approach circumvents this difficulty by a refined commutator analysis: by carefully commuting (
1.33
) with appropriately chosen
V
, and tracking the precise structure of the resulting error terms, we eliminate the need for elliptic estimates for
\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}
(and other
bad
ones) at top order. The analysis reveals an intrinsic asymmetry between the
e_{4}
and
e_{3}
directions in the Vlasov propagation (breaking the naive
e_{4}\leftrightarrow e_{3}
symmetry present in the vacuum Einstein equations modulo a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of certain Ricci coefficients), which in turn underlines the fundamental obstruction to simultaneous top–order control of Ricci coefficients on both
\mathcal{H}_{u}
and
\underline{\mathcal{H}}_{\underline{u}}
.

1.6
Reduced Elliptic Estimates

This section is devoted to the implementation of the second principal structural innovation underpinning our analysis. We uncover a novel geometric feature intrinsic to the Einstein–Vlasov system that enables the selective application of elliptic estimates to a proper subset of the null connection coefficients, specifically
(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi,\eta)
, restricted to the outgoing null hypersurface
H
. This refinement permits us to dispense entirely with elliptic control of the remaining Ricci coefficients, thereby circumventing the full elliptic theory traditionally invoked for all connection variables in energy-type arguments. We stress on the fact that this is absolutely essential since elliptic estimates for Ricci coefficients such as
\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}
causes significant obstruction that we describe towards the end of this section.

Let us introduce precise notation to describe the relevant geometric quantities. Denote by

\psi:=\{\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi,\underline{\hat{\chi}},\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi},\eta,\underline{\eta},\omega=0,\underline{\omega},\not{\Gamma}\}

the full set of Ricci coefficients (i.e., connection coefficients associated with the double null foliation), where the notation is standard and follows the conventions of the null frame formalism. The Weyl curvature components are encoded in the set

\Psi:=\{\alpha,\underline{\alpha},\beta,\underline{\beta},\rho,\sigma\},

while we denote by
\mathfrak{M}
the matter degrees of freedom associated with the Vlasov field, which include the distribution function
f
and its geometric derivatives along the fibers of the mass shell bundle.

It is a fundamental feature of the Einstein-Vlasov system that the coupling between the matter and curvature variables is nontrivial at the level of derivatives: the Bianchi equations, when written in the null frame formalism, contain terms involving first-order derivatives of the matter field
f
along the null and sphere directions. Consequently, any attempt to close an energy hierarchy for the matter variables necessarily requires control of connection coefficients at a level commensurate with that of
f
, which—owing to its transport nature in both base and momentum variables—typically requires higher regularity than the curvature components
\Psi
themselves.

The classical approach to this problem entails establishing elliptic estimates for all elements of
\psi
. This strategy ensures that one can gain sufficient regularity to estimate
f
via the Vlasov equation and simultaneously control the geometric side via the Einstein equations. At the level of energy estimates for the geometric variables, one typically seeks to estimate

\|\mathcal{D}^{N}\Psi\|_{L^{2}(H)}+\|\mathcal{D}^{N}\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\underline{H})},~\quad N\geq 1,

where
\mathcal{D}
denotes a combination of angular derivatives and projected null derivatives compatible with the geometry of the foliation. Note that the curvature components
\alpha
and
\underline{\alpha}
admit control only on the outgoing and incoming null hypersurfaces
H
and
\underline{H}
, respectively, due to their signature and the hyperbolic nature of the Bianchi system. We remark that even in this optimal setting, the current analytic techniques are insufficient to reach full
N=1
regularity for the Weyl curvature field, a point which we elaborate upon in subsequent sections.

The structure of the null Bianchi equations governing the curvature tensor requires the estimation of

\|\mathcal{D}^{N+1}T_{\mu\nu}\|_{L^{2}(H)}

where
T_{\mu\nu}
denotes the energy-momentum tensor derived from the Vlasov distribution function. Since
T_{\mu\nu}
contains velocity moments of
f
, and these moments involve integration over the mass shell coupled to the spacetime geometry, the control of
T_{\mu\nu}
at order
N+1
through the transport equation imposes stringent requirements on the differentiability of the metric coefficients. More precisely, it necessitates estimates of

\|\mathcal{D}^{N+1}\psi\|_{L^{2}(H)}+\|\mathcal{D}^{N+1}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\underline{H})}

which cannot, in general, be achieved via transport equations alone. The propagation equations for
\psi
along the null directions involve losing one derivative. As such, one must appeal to the elliptic structure inherent in the sphere-level constraint equations—specifically, the
div
–
curl
systems for
\hat{\chi}
,
\operatorname{tr}\chi
, and
\eta
—to gain the necessary derivative and close the hierarchy. This highlights the geometric necessity of invoking elliptic estimates and motivates the structural refinement we exploit in this section, which allows us to localize these elliptic estimates exclusively to the subset
(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi,\eta)
along
H
, while avoiding their application to the remaining connection variables.

However, we uncover a key structural simplification in the Einstein–Vlasov system that permits us to drastically reduce the number of connection coefficients requiring elliptic control. Specifically, we demonstrate that it suffices to derive elliptic estimates only for
(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi,\eta)
on the null hypersurface
H
. This is in sharp contrast to prior works, where elliptic control over the entire set
\psi
was deemed indispensable.

To elucidate this point, recall that in order to estimate derivatives of the Vlasov distribution function
f
, one must commute the transport equation

X[f]=0,

(1.35)

with a suitable system of vector fields
V
. Upon commutation, one obtains

X[Vf]=[X,V]f.

(1.36)

Schematically, the commutator term takes the form

[X,V]f\sim(\Psi,\psi)\cdot Vf,

(1.37)

indicating that the derivatives of both the Weyl curvature components and the connection coefficients enter at the same differential level in the evolution of
Vf
. This structural feature, specific to the Vlasov system, distinguishes it from other matter models—such as Maxwell or Yang–Mills fields—whose hyperbolic character leads to fundamentally different regularity hierarchies.

In this study, we consider
N=2
, meaning the Weyl curvature is controlled up to the second derivative. Commuting to the top order, we encounter terms of the schematic form

X[V^{3}f]\sim(\Psi,\psi)V^{3}f+\mathcal{D}^{2}(\Psi,\psi)Vf+\mathcal{D}(\Psi,\psi)V^{2}f,

(1.38)

revealing the appearance of up to second derivatives of the connection and curvature components. One might thus surmise that, as long as the curvature is controlled at second order, elliptic estimates for the connection coefficients could be dispensed with. However, the difficulty lies in certain components of
\psi
that are not expressible directly in terms of curvature and whose higher derivatives cannot be obtained without elliptic analysis.

A concrete manifestation of this obstruction is the appearance of the term
e_{A}(b^{B})
, where
b^{A}
denotes the shift vector field tangential to the sphere
S_{u,\underline{u}}
. Since this expression involves a derivative of the shift, it introduces a need to control third derivatives of
b
while estimating the desired
\|\mathcal{D}^{N+1}T_{\mu\nu}\|_{L^{2}(H)},~N=2
. From the evolution equation

\not{\nabla}_{4}b\sim(\eta-\underline{\eta})+\chi\cdot b,

(1.39)

we see that controlling
\mathcal{D}^{3}b
necessitates control over
\mathcal{D}^{3}\chi
,
\mathcal{D}^{3}\eta
, and
\mathcal{D}^{3}\underline{\eta}
. In parallel, the connection
\not{\Gamma}
satisfies the equation

\not{\nabla}_{4}\not{\Gamma}\sim\not{\nabla}\chi+\not{\Gamma}\cdot\chi,

(1.40)

which again involves third derivatives of
\chi
. These considerations suggest that elliptic control over
(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi,\eta,\underline{\eta})
is required. But the elliptic estimates for
\underline{\eta}
require elliptic estimates for
\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}
and
\underline{\chi}
, which in turn require elliptic estimates for
\underline{\omega}
. Therefore, it appears one needs elliptic estimates for all the connection coefficients.

However, a key observation allows us to avoid this conclusion.
The potentially dangerous term
e_{A}(b^{B})
never appears in isolation.
Instead, it always appears in the combination

\Gamma^{B}_{3A}={\underline{\chi}_{A}}^{B}-e_{A}(b^{B}),

(1.41)

that is, as a component of the connection in the null frame.
Let us give a concrete examples of how this term appears. For the geodesic vector field
X:=p^{\mu}e_{\mu}-\Gamma^{\overline{\lambda}}_{\alpha\beta}p^{\alpha}p^{\beta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{p}^{\lambda}},\lambda=1,2,3
and a vector field
V_{A}:=\text{Hor}(e_{A})-\frac{p^{3}}{|u|}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{p}^{A}}
, if one computes
[X,V_{A}]
, then semi-schematically

\displaystyle[X,V_{A}]=\Bigg(-p^{3}e_{A}(b^{B})+\Gamma^{B}_{A\nu}p^{\nu}+\frac{p^{3}}{|u|}\delta^{B}_{A}\Bigg)V_{B}+....

(1.42)

Notice the appearance of
e_{A}(b^{B})
in the commutation as the error term (and it appears in the exact same fashion everywhere else). But we can write the full error term
-p^{3}e_{A}(b^{B})+\Gamma^{B}_{A\nu}p^{\nu}+\frac{p^{3}}{|u|}\delta^{B}_{A}
as follows

\displaystyle-p^{3}e_{A}(b^{B})+\Gamma^{B}_{A\nu}p^{\nu}+\frac{p^{3}}{|u|}\delta^{B}_{A}=\underbrace{\Gamma^{B}_{A3}p^{3}+\frac{p^{3}}{|u|}\delta^{B}_{A}-p^{3}e_{A}(b^{B})}_{I}+\Gamma^{B}_{A4}p^{4}+\not{\Gamma}^{B}_{AC}p^{C}.

(1.43)

Now consider the term
I
(the remaining terms have good enough integrability), which involves a non-integrable term
\frac{p^{3}}{|u|}
(note that
p^{3}
does not decay) and the term involving the shift vector field. First, recall the following

\displaystyle\Gamma^{B}_{A3}=\Gamma^{B}_{3A}+e_{A}(b^{B}),

(1.44)

which yields cancellation of the term
-p^{3}e_{A}(b^{B})
. One can proceed to obtain an equation for
\Gamma^{B}_{3A}
. Denoting
\Gamma^{B}_{3A}
by
\mathcal{G}^{B}_{A}
(technically speaking we will control
\Gamma[g]^{B}_{3A}-\Gamma[\eta]^{B}_{3A}
since
\Gamma^{B}_{3A}
is not a tensor rather can be interpreted as an endomorphism of the tangent bundle of the sphere
S_{u,\underline{u}}
), schematically, such an equation reads

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\mathcal{G}\sim\not{\nabla}\eta+\mathcal{G}\cdot\chi+\underline{\omega}\cdot\chi+\not{\Gamma}\cdot(\eta,\underline{\eta})+\eta\cdot\underline{\eta}+(\rho,\sigma)+\not{T},

(1.45)

where one notices that the only derivative term that appears is
\not{\nabla}\eta
. This hints that one may estimate
\mathcal{G}
by the elliptic estimates for
\eta
(and
(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi)
since they appear for the elliptic estimates for
\eta
). However, one still needs to take care of the non-integrable term
\frac{p^{3}}{|u|}
. To this end, we make the following set of manipulations

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{A}_{B}=\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{A}_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}[\mathcal{G}]\delta^{A}_{B},

(1.46)

where explicitly

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{A}_{B}=\widehat{\Gamma}^{A}_{B3}-[e_{A}(b^{B})-\frac{1}{2}e_{C}(b^{C})\delta^{A}_{B}],~\operatorname{tr}[\mathcal{G}]=\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}-e_{C}(b^{C})

(1.47)

and note that

\displaystyle p^{3}\Gamma^{A}_{B3}-e_{A}(b^{B})p^{3}+\frac{p^{3}}{|u|}=

\displaystyle p^{3}\Bigg(\widehat{\Gamma}^{A}_{B3}-[e_{A}(b^{B})-\frac{1}{2}e_{C}(b^{C})\delta^{A}_{B}]+\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{2}{|u|}-e_{C}(b^{C}))\delta^{A}_{B}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{3}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{A}_{B}+\frac{1}{2}p^{3}\Bigg(\operatorname{tr}[\mathcal{G}]+\frac{2}{|u|}\Bigg)\delta^{A}_{B}.

(1.48)

Schematically, the equation for
\operatorname{tr}[\mathcal{G}]+\frac{2}{|u|}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
reads

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\Bigg(tr[\mathcal{G}]+\frac{2}{|u|}\Bigg)\sim

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}\hskip 1.42262pt\widehat{\mathcal{G}}+\mbox{div }\eta+|\eta|^{2}+\eta\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{\eta}+\operatorname{tr}\chi\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}+\underline{\omega}\hskip 1.42262pt\operatorname{tr}\chi+\hat{\chi}\hskip 1.42262pt\underline{\hat{\chi}}+\not{\Gamma}(\eta,\underline{\eta})+(\rho,\sigma)+\not{T},

(1.49)

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}\sim

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}\eta+\widehat{\mathcal{G}}\cdot\chi+\chi\chi+\underline{\omega}\hskip 1.42262pt\chi+\not{\Gamma}\cdot(\eta,\underline{\eta})+\eta(\eta,\underline{\eta})+(\rho,\sigma)+\not{T}.

(1.50)

Consequently, controlling
\mathcal{D}^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{G}},\hskip 1.42262pt\mathcal{D}^{2}(tr[\mathcal{G}]+\frac{2}{|u|})
in
L^{2}(S)
requires only control over
\mathcal{D}^{3}\eta
along
H
, together with lower-order control over the remaining terms. This is the central insight: The entire analytic burden reduces to deriving elliptic estimates for
\eta
along
H
, eliminating the need for such estimates on
(\underline{\hat{\chi}},\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi},\underline{\omega},\underline{\eta})
. We now illustrate how we can decouple the elliptic estimates for
\eta
from those of
\underline{\eta}
and subsequently
(\underline{\hat{\chi}},\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi},\underline{\omega})
.

To estimate
\eta
, one invokes elliptic estimates associated to
(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi)
. Crucially, the elliptic estimates for
(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi)
do not propagate derivatives in the incoming null direction, and are therefore entirely compatible with working on
H
alone. Thus, we are able to establish full regularity for the matter distribution
f
, up to the requisite top order, while performing elliptic estimates exclusively for
(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi,\eta)
along
H
. However, there is a subtlety in the elliptic estimates for
\eta
. Since the equation for
\eta
contains Weyl curvature
\beta
, one can not directly use this transport equation. Instead, the standard approach is to define the mass aspect function

\displaystyle\mu:=-\mbox{div }\eta-\rho+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\hat{\chi}}

(1.51)

which, upon being acted on by
\not{\nabla}_{4}
and through the use of the equation for
\rho
, eliminates the dangerous
\not{\nabla}\beta
term. In the current framework, however, this is not enough to decouple
\eta
and
\underline{\eta}
since
\mbox{div }(\nabla_{4}\eta)
would contain terms
\chi\cdot\not{\nabla}\underline{\eta}
potentially obstructing this route. To this end, we define a new
\mu
as follows

\displaystyle\mu:=-\mbox{div }\eta-\rho+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\hat{\chi}}+\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{tr}\chi\widetilde{\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}}

(1.52)

which precisely cancels out the extra
\not{\nabla}\underline{\eta}
term:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{4}\mu\sim(\eta,\underline{\eta})\not{\nabla}(\hat{\chi},\operatorname{tr}\chi)+\hat{\chi}\not{\nabla}\eta+\not{\nabla}_{4}T_{43}+\not{\nabla}_{3}T_{44}+l.o.t.

(1.53)

This, together with the equation for
\mbox{curl }\eta
, immediately yields the top elliptic estimate for
\eta
without the need for elliptic estimates for
\underline{\eta}
. Since the elliptic estimates for
\underline{\eta}
are unnecessary in this context, one avoids having to establish the elliptic estimates for
\underline{\hat{\chi}},\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi},
and
\underline{\omega}
, which would have to be obtained first. In addition, there would be serious complications in working with elliptic estimates for
\underline{\hat{\chi}}
and
\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}
in the present framework, where the Vlasov matter source is only estimated on
H
at the top order. This is because of the structure of the equation
\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{3}\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi})^{2}=-|\hat{\chi}|^{2}-2\,\underline{\omega}\,\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}-T_{33}

(1.54)

Note that controlling
\not{\nabla}^{3}\operatorname{tr}\underline{\chi}
over
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
would require controlling the topmost order norm
\not{\nabla}^{3}T_{33}
over
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
which is impossible. Therefore, without this aforementioned reduction in elliptic estimates, this program is doomed to fail.

This reduction in analytic complexity—made possible by careful exploitation of the geometric structure of the Einstein–Vlasov system—constitutes a key technical innovation of our work and, to the best of our knowledge, has not been previously observed in the literature.

1.7
Optimal regularity

A central aspect of the present analysis is that it is conducted at the optimal threshold of regularity for the coupled Einstein–Vlasov system. Specifically, the level of differentiability employed in our framework is optimal, in the sense that any attempt to lower it would render the nonlinear estimates intractable. To contextualize this, we recall the seminal contribution of Klainerman and Rodnianski
[
19
]
, who established control over one derivative of the Weyl curvature tensor in the scale-invariant norm
\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(H/\penalty\underline{H})
in their proof of semi-global existence and the dynamical formation of trapped surfaces in vacuum spacetimes. Their analysis crucially relied on refined trace inequalities on codimension-two submanifolds, which were employed to circumvent the borderline
failure
of the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}(S)\not\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(S)
in two spatial dimensions, where
S
denotes a spacelike 2-sphere. In the present section, we shall demonstrate that the same regularity level, while sufficient for controlling the vacuum Einstein equations (and in the presence of some sources such as Maxwell, Yang-Mills, or scalar field), presents new obstructions in the context of the Einstein–Vlasov system. These difficulties arise from the interplay between the distributional nature of the Vlasov matter field and the null geometry of the spacetime, which amplifies the necessity for sharper control on both the phase space density and the associated geometric quantities.

Let us now outline the structural mechanism that underpins the analysis in the coupled Einstein–Vlasov system, particularly as it pertains to the propagation of regularity in a double null foliation. To this end, we recall the codimension-two trace norms introduced by Klainerman–Rodnianski, which are designed to capture the borderline behavior of geometric quantities on null hypersurfaces in a scale-invariant manner. Given a tensor field
\varphi\in\Gamma({}^{K}\otimes TS_{u,\underline{u}})
, the trace norms on the outgoing and incoming null hypersurfaces
H_{u}
and
\underline{H}_{\underline{u}}
, respectively, are defined by

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{\operatorname{tr}(H)}:=\sup_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\underline{u}}|\varphi|^{2}\,d\underline{u}^{\prime}\right)^{1/2},\qquad\|\varphi\|_{\operatorname{tr}(\underline{H})}:=\sup_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\left(\int_{0}^{u}|\varphi|^{2}\,du^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}.

(1.55)

These norms are essential for controlling curvature components in low regularity settings, particularly in light of the failure of the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}(S)\not\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(S)
in two dimensions. Suppose we aim to estimate one angular derivative of the Weyl curvature tensor in the scale-invariant energy space
\mathcal{L}^{2}_{sc}(H/\penalty\underline{H})
. Then, due to the schematic structure of the null Bianchi identities, up to two angular derivatives of the matter stress-energy tensor must be controlled.

In the Einstein–Vlasov system, the matter stress-energy tensor is defined as a velocity average over the Vlasov distribution function
f
, and thus controlling two angular derivatives of the stress-energy tensor necessitates controlling two angular derivatives of
f
. Since the Vlasov field evolves along the geodesic flow in phase space, the regularity must be propagated along null geodesics in the spacetime. At this top order, the key structural constraint is the fact that only the regularity of
f
along the outgoing null hypersurface
H_{u_{\infty}}
can be effectively propagated into the bulk via integration along the incoming null geodesics.

To quantify this, observe that for a null geodesic
s\mapsto\gamma(s)
, the ratio between the momentum component
p^{3}(s)
and the derivative of the eikonal function
u
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{p^{3}(s)}{\frac{du}{ds}}=1.

(1.56)

Here,
u(s):=u(\gamma(s))
, and the bound follows from the normalization condition on null geodesics and the support properties of the distribution function in phase space. This estimate ensures that the geodesic flow does not excessively distort the geometric foliation, allowing us to integrate the Vlasov equation along incoming null directions. Consequently, at the highest derivative level (corresponding to the critical second derivative of
f
), the estimates must be propagated along the incoming generators from the initial data specified on
H_{u_{\infty}}
, since the regularity cannot be recovered directly from transverse directions without incurring derivative loss.

We now illustrate the principal difficulty through a concrete model computation, which encapsulates the essential mechanism by which curvature components couple to the Vlasov field at the top derivative level. Let
f\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
denote the distribution function on phase space, supported on the future mass shell and solving the transport equation associated to the geodesic spray vector field
X
, i.e.,

\displaystyle Xf=0.

(1.57)

Commuting this equation with a suitably chosen differential operator
V
, adapted to the geometric structure (e.g., angular derivatives in spacetime lifted horizontally to the mass shell), yields the schematic identities

\displaystyle X[Vf]

\displaystyle\sim\alpha\cdot\not{p}\cdot Vf+\text{l.o.t.},

(1.58)

\displaystyle X[V^{2}f]

\displaystyle\sim(\mathcal{D}\alpha)\cdot\not{p}\cdot Vf+\alpha\cdot V^{2}f+\text{l.o.t.},

(1.59)

where
\alpha
denotes the outgoing null curvature component,
\mathcal{D}\alpha
its angular derivative, and
\not{p}
the angular projection of the momentum variable.

To estimate the top-order (second angular derivative) energy of
f
, we integrate the square of
V^{2}f
over phase space fibers
\mathcal{P}_{x}\subset T^{*}_{x}\mathcal{M}
, spacetime spheres
S_{u,\underline{u}}
, and along the incoming foliation in
\underline{u}\in[0,1]
, yielding the energy-type quantity

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}|V^{2}f|^{2}\,d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\,d\mu_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\,d\underline{u}.

(1.60)

Upon integrating the commuted transport equation, the leading-order error term arising from the source
\mathcal{D}\alpha\cdot\not{p}\cdot Vf
takes the form

\displaystyle II:=\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}V^{2}f\cdot(\mathcal{D}\alpha)\cdot\not{p}\cdot Vf\,d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\,d\mu_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\,d\underline{u}\,du.

(1.61)

To estimate
II
, observe that by the structure of the spacetime hierarchy and the null curvature equations,
\mathcal{D}\alpha\in L^{2}(H)
but no stronger norm is available at this regularity level. Therefore, we are compelled to control the remaining integrand terms in
L^{\infty}(S_{u,\underline{u}})L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{x})
, specifically

\displaystyle\sup_{\underline{u}\in[0,1]}\sup_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}|Vf|^{2}\,d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\right)^{1/2}.

(1.62)

This is indeed sharp, as the curvature component
\mathcal{D}\alpha
cannot be controlled beyond
L^{2}
, and any attempt to gain stronger pointwise bounds would violate the optimality of the regularity framework.

Accordingly, the control of the second-order energy of
f
reduces to establishing uniform control on the first-order energy

\displaystyle E_{1}(u,\underline{u}):=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}|Vf|^{2}\,d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}},

(1.63)

uniformly over
S_{u,\underline{u}}
and
\underline{u}\in[0,1]
. To propagate this control from initial data, one integrates the first-order commuted transport equation. The associated energy identity introduces the following error term, arising from the interaction of
\alpha\cdot\not{p}
with
|Vf|^{2}
:

\displaystyle III:=\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\alpha\cdot\not{p}\cdot|Vf|^{2}\,d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\,du.

(1.64)

This term reflects the borderline nature of the coupling and reveals the necessity of maintaining uniform control on
E_{1}
, as the curvature coefficient
\alpha
again resides only in a scale-invariant space and cannot be controlled pointwise.

Since the curvature component
\alpha
cannot be controlled in
L^{\infty}
, one might attempt to estimate the error term

\displaystyle III:=\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\alpha\cdot\not{p}\cdot|Vf|^{2}\,d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}\,du

(1.65)

by invoking the refined codimension-two trace estimates of Klainerman–Rodnianski, together with a priori bounds on the momentum support of
f
. Specifically, one applies the estimate

\displaystyle|III|\lesssim\sup_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\left(\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}|\alpha|^{2}\,du^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}|u|^{-2}\,du^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}\sup_{u}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}|Vf|^{2}\,d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}},

(1.66)

which, under the assumption of finite momentum support and scale-invariant geometric control, simplifies to

\displaystyle|III|\lesssim\|\alpha\|_{\operatorname{tr}(\underline{H})}\cdot|u|^{-1/2}\cdot\sup_{u}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}|Vf|^{2}\,d\mu_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}.

(1.67)

At first glance, this appears to furnish the requisite estimate for controlling the error term
III
. However, the fundamental obstruction lies in the fact that
\alpha
cannot be controlled in the trace norm
\operatorname{tr}(\underline{H})
. Indeed, in the hierarchy of curvature estimates established in the double null foliation framework, the component
\alpha
- which corresponds to the null-null projection of the Weyl tensor in the outgoing direction- exhibits the highest singular behavior and resides only in
L^{2}(H)
, but not in any norm permitting its trace along incoming hypersurfaces
\underline{H}
.

Consequently, the trace norm
\|\alpha\|_{\operatorname{tr}(\underline{H})}
is not finite in general, and the above strategy fails to yield a closed energy estimate. This leads to a critical barrier: one cannot establish control over the second angular derivative of the Vlasov distribution function
f
while working with only first derivative bounds on the curvature tensor. That is, the system fails to close at the optimal regularity threshold expected for the characteristic initial value problem; namely, two derivatives on
f
and one derivative on the Weyl curvature.

This problem is circumvented if one works with two derivatives of the Weyl curvature. In this setting we have
L^{\infty}(S)
control of the Riemann curvature, since
L^{\infty}(S)
of the Weyl curvature can be controlled by means of the
L^{4}(S)
norm of its first derivatives, modulo lower derivative terms. This in turn can be estimated by the
L^{2}(H)
of its second derivatives by means of the codimension-1 trace inequality. Naturally, we need to control the three derivatives of the Vlasov in
L^{2}(H)L^{2}(\mathcal{P}_{x})
. Consider the vector field
V_{3}
for simplicity. The norms that we need to control associated with this vector field are as follows

A:=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}|V_{3}f|^{2}d\mathcal{P}_{x},

(1.68)

B:=\int_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}|V^{2}_{3}f|^{2}

(1.69)

C:=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}|V^{3}_{3}f|^{2}.

(1.70)

Now consider the most difficult term
C
. By the transport equation,

\displaystyle C(u)\leq C(u_{\infty})+2\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{S_{u,\underline{u}}}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{x}}V^{3}_{3}fX[V^{3}f]+\text{Gronwall~terms}

(1.71)

Now schematically

\displaystyle X[V_{3}f]\sim\alpha_{AB}p^{A}p^{B}V_{3}f+....

(1.72)

and so

\displaystyle X[V^{3}f]\sim V^{2}_{3}(\alpha Vf)+....\sim\underbrace{\alpha_{AB}p^{A}p^{B}V^{3}f}_{I}+\underbrace{\mathcal{D}\alpha_{AB}p^{A}p^{B}V^{2}_{3}f}_{II}+\underbrace{\mathcal{D}^{2}\alpha_{AB}p^{A}p^{B}V_{3}f}_{III}+\dots

(1.73)

We have three cases:

•

Terms in bracket
I:
Controlled by putting
\alpha
in
L^{\infty}(S_{u,\underline{u}})
.

•

Terms in bracket
III:
Controlled by bounding
\mathcal{D}^{2}\alpha
in the energy norm and by a uniform estimate on
V_{3}f
, through uniform control of
A
over all
S_{u,\underline{u}}
.

•

Terms in bracket
II:
This case is the most difficult one and requires careful treatment. Note the following computations
